RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 118 OF 124 
 H 
horticultural crops, these systems also 
contain tree components that provide both 
service and production roles. The diversity 
of tree species is similar to home gardens and spice gardens.  
Analog forests model the process of natural 
forest development and forest service 
functions in (re)establishing a sustainable 
ecosystem characterized by a high 
biodiversity to biomass ratio. Analog forests 
are designed through a synthesis of 
traditional and scientific knowledge, to 
optimize the production and service 
potential of the system rather than to 
maximize the productivity of a single species component. While focused on 
ecosystem restoration, analog forests are 
also designed to provide economic 
benefits.  Annual species are also components of analog forests.  
Home gardens, spice gardens, annual 
horticultural systems, and analog forests 
can be viewed as a continuum of smallholder systems. From horticultural 
systems focused on annual crops, to home 
gardens and spice gardens representing 
perennial (tree) farming systems, to analog 
forests being a forest management and 
restoration approach. All include both 
annual and perennial species and are 
concerned with sustainable management. 
A difference being that home gardens, 
spice gardens and annual horticultural 
systems focus on enhancing smallholder 
livelihoods in an environmentally 
sustainable manner; and analog forests focus on ecosystem restoration with 
livelihoods as the secondary objective.  spacing, remove 
unproductive trees, remove 
low value species, and 
increase the vigor and 
productivity of the remnant 
stand; 
 Promoting pruning to 
remove unproductive 
branches, improve tree 
vigor and productivity, and 
increase light levels for 
understory intercrops;  
 Promoting intercropping 
with annual crops to 
improve overall system 
productivity;  
 Promoting the production 
and use of organic mulch 
and fertilizers to rehabilitate 
soils, improve water 
recharge, and produce 
products for the green 
economy;  
 Exploring the feasibility of 
drip irrigation for high-value 
tree crops to improve water 
use efficiency and increase 
tree vigor and productivity.  
 
1.2.3: Restoration and 
intensification of sustainable plantations This will include tea, coconut, rubber, 
timber and large-scale cultivation of spices 
and include development of landscape 
planning at estate level and the 
development of food forests to address 
food security of estate worker families, 
some of whom have entered contract 
farming arrangements with estate 
companies.   
 Timber plantations in or near natural forest 
areas will be included in 1.1.3 while those 
in predominantly agricultural landscapes 
will be covered here.  
  Landscape planning of 
estates to allocate land 
appropriately to intensive 
cultivation of export crops, 
subsistence production of 
nutritionally rich diets for 
estate workers and their 
families and soil and water 
conservation, including the 
establishment of exemplar 
landscapes. 
 Species suitability mapping 
to reduce climate, 
biophysical and market

branches, improve tree 
vigor and productivity, and 
increase light levels for 
understory intercrops;  
 Promoting intercropping 
with annual crops to 
improve overall system 
productivity;  
 Promoting the production 
and use of organic mulch 
and fertilizers to rehabilitate 
soils, improve water 
recharge, and produce 
products for the green 
economy;  
 Exploring the feasibility of 
drip irrigation for high-value 
tree crops to improve water 
use efficiency and increase 
tree vigor and productivity.

3.1.1: Develop an integrated 
land use policy and planning 
mechanism at sub-basin scale To carry out this activity and ensure long 
term sustainability, three new governance 
structures (implementation teams) will be 
developed that will be put in place to 
integrate across non-congruent 
administrative (Divisional Secretariat - DS) 
and hydrological (sub-catchment) 
boundaries  
1. The five sub-catchments on the east 
of the target catchment area (Amban 
Ganga, Puwakpitiya Oya, Thelgamu 
Oya, Kalu Ganga and Knuckles 
Ellewana Kanda Eastern Slope). 
These are well connected to one another and embrace the large DSs of 
Lagalla Pallegama and Wilgamuwa 
and the larger eastern part of the 
Naula DS (the rest is part of 3 below). 
Much of the area is less degraded 
than other parts of the overall 
catchment so the density of 
intervention is anticipated to be lighter 
than in other sub-basin units.  
2. The upland part of the Sudu Ganga 
sub-catchment including the Rattota and Amban Ganga Korale DSs. 
3. The lower part of the Sudu Ganga 
sub-catchment including the Ukuwela, 
Matale and parts of Akurana and 
Naula DSs   
4. The Nalanda Oya sub-catchment 
embracing Pallepola and Yatawatta. 
5. The Heen Ganga and Hasalaka Oya 
sub-catchments in the south, 
comprising Udadumbara DS and part 
of Minipe  DS. Three sub-basin SHARED 
stakeholder engagement 
processes  Three integrated  SHARED 
stakeholder engagement 
processes covering three sub-
basin structures  comprised of  
multisector information 
systems and multi-stakeholder 
engagement and planning 
mechanism  
A financial and resource 
mechanism in place to support 
the operation of the three sub-
basin governance schemes 
consisting of Government, 
Private Sector, Farmer 
Organizations and other 
relevant actors.  
A support platform that uses 
digital technologies, modern 
communications and other 
tools for integrated land use 
planning and implementation 
including incentive systems for 
best practices.  
3.1.2 Develop a shared

adaptation, market information 
and monitoring of the 
performance of intervention 
options The information system will involve 
developing spatially explicit assessments of 
land degradation that are critical for the 
development of effective adaptation options 
and targeting interventions, while also

progress over time.  Both socio-economic 
and biophysical information will be

with associated satellite 
image analysis

dashboards

to connect sub-basins to an 
integrated upstream 
catchment portal 
 
 
 
 
 
 By providing a robust indicator 
framework, advanced analytics 
and diagnostics (models), the 
information system will be key 
to the development of spatially 
explicit interventions to protect 
and restore ecosystem function 
and adaptation. Land 
degradation hotspots (e.g. soil 
erosion and compaction) and 
soil health variables (e.g. soil 
organic carbon) will be mapped 
at high spatial resolution (10 to 
30m), by combining data 
collected from the LDSF sites 
with data from the global 
database.

H.2. Arrangements for Monito ring, Reporting and Evaluation  
 
331. Project level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the IUCN 2015 
Monitoring and Evaluation Policy, Accreditati on Master Agreement (AMA) between GCF and  
IUCN and the IUCN Access to Information Policy.   
 
332. Project monitoring will measure achievement of the performance indicators and report on the 
implementation progress of GCF project while it  is being implemented. Performance indicators 
are identified according to the principles est ablished in the GCF Initial Results Management 
Framework linking Time-bound sets of activities to a set of agreed adaptation results. IUCN 
maintains a standard list of technically well-informed indicators aligned to its donors core 
indicators, including GCF. IUCN aims to develop its standard indicators for each programmatic area with afferent measurement tools to ensure effective data collection and harmonized 
reporting. IUCN’s Programme and Project Portal allows efficient and timely data management and 
project information disclosure   
 
333. A project inception workshop will be held afte r the project document is signed by all relevant 
parties to: a) re-orient project stakeholders to t he project strategy and discuss any changes in the 
overall context that influence project implementatio n; b) discuss the roles and responsibilities of 
the project team, including reporting and communication lines and conflict resolution mechanisms; c) review the results framework, re-assess baselin es as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring 
and evaluation roles and responsibilities and finaliz e the M&E plan; d) review financial reporting 
procedures and mandatory requirements, and agr ee on the arrangements for the annual audit; e) 
plan and schedule Project Board meetings and final ize the first year annual work plan. IUCN will 
prepare the inception report no later than one mo nth after the inception workshop. The final 
inception report will be cleared by the National  Steering Committee before submission to the GCF 
Secretariat.  
 
 
334. A Project Implementation Report (PIR) will be pr epared for each year of pr oject implementation. 
Each report is cleared by the National St eering Committee before submission to the GCF 
Secretariat. The Programme Director of the project, the IUCN Country Office, and the IUCN 
Regional Technical Advisor will provide objective input to the annual PIR. The Programme Director 
will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored annually 
well in advance of the implementation report submission deadline and will objectively report 
progress. The annual implementation report will be shared with the National Steering Committee 
and other stakeholders. IUCN will coordinate the input of the NDA Focal Point and other 
stakeholders to the implementation report. The final  project implementation report, along with the 
terminal evaluation report, will serve as the final project report package.  
 
 
335. Project mid-term (MTE) and final (FE) eval uations will be commissioned and managed by the 
IUCN Planning, Monitoring, Evaluation and Risk Unit – IUCN’s independent evaluation office.  
Independent evaluators under the oversight of  PMER will be contracted to conduct these 
evaluations. The evaluation criter ia are aligned to the GCF IEU Evaluation Criteria. The MTE will 
assess (1) the relevance, effectiveness, effici ency and likelihood of impact and sustainability, (2) 
the coherence in climate finance delivery, (3) gend er equity and social inclusion (minorities), (3) 
and country ownership. The FE will assess (1) the relevance, effectiveness, efficiency and 
likelihood of impact and sustainability, (2) the co herence in climate finance delivery, (3) gender 
equity and social inclusion (minorities), (3) and c ountry ownership, (4) innovativeness in result 
areas, and (6) replication and sc alability. The evaluation will appl y a process tracing methodology. 
Data collection will include stakeholder interviews, site visits and documentary reviews. The IUCN 
mana gement response will be reviewed b y the National Steerin g Committee. Evaluation reports


---
*Page 127*
---

 
RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 125 OF 124 
 H 
and management responses will be made available to the public through the IUCN Evaluations 
Database . 
  
 336. The IUCN Sri Lanka office will retain all M&E records for this project for up to six years after project 
financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations. A detailed M&E budget, monitoring plan 
and evaluation plan will be included in the proposal and ESM framework.  
 
337. In addition to the above, National Steering Committee will be gathered quarterly or more often 
than that as per the requirement and m onitor the progress against the annual project 
implementation plan. The Executive Entities whic h are IUCN, Ministry of Mahaweli Development 
and Environment and ICRAF will be responsible to l ead the presentations of the progress for the 
out puts and activities they are responsible. Under the facilitation of  the EE the contracted 
organizations for each sub activities (that are in line with the project TOC and the budget) will be 
presented the progress. Any bo ttlenecks will be exposed at this  forum and the Na tional Steering 
Committee will provide or get immediate actions to the internally and externally popped up issues.

Proposal name: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Subsistence Farmers and 
Agricultural Plantation Communities residing in the vulnerable 
river basins, watershed areas and downstream of the Knuckles 
Mountain Range Catchment of Sri Lanka

I. Overall	assessment 	of	the	Secretariat 	
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Bo ard for consideration with the following 
remarks:

lessons learned from other practices1 aimed 
at increasing water availability, intensive 
cropping techniques and sustainable 
financing schemes Aims to achieve horizontal and vertical

II. Summary 	of	the	Secretariat’s 	assessment 	
2.1 Project background  
3. The “Strengthening climate resilience of su bsistence farmers and agricultural plantation 
communities residing in the vulnerable river basins, watershed areas and downstream of the Knuckles Mountain Range catchment of Sri Lanka” project aims to strengthen the adaptive 
 
1 Namely, the MetaMeta initiative in the Netherlands; the Forest Landscape Restoration Mechanism approach, such as 
the International Union for Conservation of Nature/World Resource Institute developed Restoration Opportunities Assessment Methodology, the System of Rice Intensification, and the Payment for Ecosystem Services Mechanism.


---
*Page 134*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 134 
 
 
capacity of smallholder subsistence farmers to address climate-induced ir rigation and drinking 
water shortages by improving the resilience of  farm- and land-management practices, and by 
climate proofing the underlying ecosystems in the Knuckles/Amban Ganga highlands and lowlands. The interventions are focused on: 	
(a) Promoting sustainable land use and profitabil ity of farming and plantation management 
in upstream areas, as well as the efficien t use of irrigation water and climate-adapted 
agriculture downstream; 	
(b) Implementing sustainable financing to enable investment in catchment protection and 
sustainable land use through value-chain upgrading and the establishment of payment 
for ecosystem service schemes, respectively; and 	
(c) Providing institutional support to deve lop appropriate governance mechanisms, 
information systems and rural advisory servic es capable of delivering appropriate land 
use options across the project area. 	
4. The project area covers the upper waters hed and downstream areas of the Knuckles 
Mountain Range, which is a United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization 
Heritage Site that includes key biodiversity hotspots and forest reserves. The region is 
dominated by tea plantations and forests (natural and plantation) along with other field crops and home gardens. The water absorbed and released is used for power generation, agriculture 
within the mountain and downstream areas, and domestic consumption. The reservoirs in the 
upstream catchment are the primary source of  irrigation water for agriculture and other 
livelihoods in the downstream command area comprising 506,260 ha, of which 122,150 ha are 
covered in irrigated rice. 	
5. Increases in temperature and windspeed as well as a shift in the intensity of seasonal 
rainfall are observed to accelerate the intensity and frequency of extreme events, such as floods, 
landslides and droughts that are erosive. Increased erosion and sedimentation in the upstream 
catchments reduce the reservoir capacity as we ll as soil quality, in turn, increasing food 
insecurity for smallholder and subsistence farmer s in the downstream area where irrigation 
supply is inadequate for agricultural practices. 	
6. The total project finance needed is USD 48.9 75 million, with a request to GCF for grant 
finance of USD 39.775 million (81 per cent).  The Ministry of Maha weli Development and 
Environment (MMDE) is contributing USD 5.9 million  in the form of grants, in addition to USD 3 
million and USD 0.3 million in the form of grants contributed through the Mahaweli Authority of 
Sri Lanka and Forest Department, respectively. 	
2.2 Component-by-component analysis 
Component 	1:	Climate‐resilient	sustainable 	land	management 	(total	cost:	USD	21.72	million;	GCF	
cost:	USD	16.74	million)	
7. This component aims to improve land and water management with two key outputs: 
(a) Safeguarding the production of environmen tal goods and services in the upstream 
catchment area: the interventions to achieve th is output will build on providing relevant 
information on climate change, land and water management practices to farmers and other land users in the upstream catchment area as well as reducing sedimentation that 
decreases water availability fo r downstream irrigation; and 
(b) Increasing the productivity and climate r esilience of primary production in both 
upstream catchment and downstre am irrigated agricultural areas: this will be achieved 
through supporting the local stakeholders and land users, including farmers, foresters


---
*Page 135*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 135 
 
 
and plantation companies/workers, to adopt efficient water-use and irrigation 
techniques.  
8. The proposed interventions under this comp onent take an integrated approach, where 
the downstream catchment area is to benefit th rough interventions in the upstream catchment 
area, by ensuring a continuum between upstream and downstream hydrological and ecosystem 
benefits.  
Component 	2:	Secure	financing 	mechanisms 	for	sustainable 	land	management 	(total	cost:	USD	
12.96	million;	GCF	cost:	USD	10.46	million)	
9. This component focuses on sustaining improved land management through the 
establishment of sustainable financing. Interv entions under this component include upgrading 
green value chains with increased efficiency and linkages to new markets and establishing a 
payment for ecosystem services (PES) mechanism. 
10. The proposed interventions are expected to enhance the sustainable outcomes of 
interventions under component 1 by establishing systems that create business cases and ensure 
profitability of sustainable land use and farming.  Detailed mapping of agricultural value chains 
and regular market analysis will be conducted to provide the basis for value-chain upgrading 
options for the smallholder and subsistence farmers in the upstream catchment area. Through the establishment of value-chain i nnovation platforms, the project will set up “soft” institutions 
where needed, such as cooperatives; innovation  platforms; producer, agri-business and trade 
associations; or chambers of commerce overseas. 
11. Building upon lessons learned from other Asian countries, the establishment of a PES 
mechanism is expected to provide financial ince ntives for the continuance of climate adaptive 
catchment protection practices. It will involve establishing an adaptive rural advisory service 
facility to provide technical support through sharing knowledge and real-time information on 
climate risks as well as best-fit options for the local context. A series of capacity strengthening 
activities will be conducted for local stakeholders to strengthen their capacity to monitor, 
evaluate and develop further relevant criteria an d indicators, when there is a need to modify 
existing, or establish new, PES schemes in the future. 
Component 	3:	Strengthen 	institutional 	capacity	(total	cost:	USD	12.23	million;	GCF	cost:	USD	10.98	
million)	
12. This component aims to address the existing constraints in governance structure as well 
as the hydrological boundaries. Interventions will promote an integrated governance 
mechanism through increased coordination of  expertise across different ministries and 
departments to support the development of land use policy, planning and intervention options 
at the subbasin level. Multi-stakeholder innovati on platforms, set up at the subbasin level, will 
facilitate such horizontal as well as vertical integration. 
13. Under this component, the project aims to improve land use policy and planning with 
increased understanding of the local context,  through multi-stakeholder consultations. 
Information will be shared through online dashbo ards developed to support the identification 
and implementation of appropriate adaptive options in sustainable land use and management. To this end, training guides, guidelines and tools will be developed. 
Project	management 	(total	cost:	USD	2.1	million;	GCF	cost:	USD	1.6	million)	
14. GCF grant financing represents 77 per cent of the total project management costs. The 
requested project management costs amount to 4.19 per cent of the total grant requested from 
GCF.

III. Assessment 	of	performance 	against	investment 	criteria		
3.1 Impact potential                                                             Scale:	Medium‐High  
15. The proposed project has multiple impacts at  the project sites and important additional 
benefits downstream of the project area. In the project area, current unsustainable land use 
practices will be made climate resilient through improved land use planning and management and the introduction of land use practices that are more responsive to climate change. Past management practices favoured the clearing of trees on agricultural lands and mono-cropping 
of annual crops, leading to severe land degrad ation and erosion, and siltation in reservoirs 
below the highlands. The project will introduc e improved land use practices, based on 
indigenous perennials, leading to a reversal of  land degradation, a reduction in erosion and 
siltation in downstream reservoirs, and an increased streamflow for a longer period throughout 
the year, which will support irrigat ed cropping in the lowlands. 
16. The project will build institutional capacity in sustainable land management in 
government agencies at local and national levels. This increased capacity will lead to sustained 
support for sustainable land management after the closure of the project. 
3.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                        Scale:	High  
17. The project area is characterized by large commercial estates – mostly tea plantations – 
and small-scale farms that produce annual crops.  Both the estates and the farms employ land 
use practices that are geared towards mechanizatio n (removal of trees, upscaling), even though 
the geography of the area is not amenable to such practices and it increases vulnerability to climate change. The project will introduce a form of productive land use based on the local 
ecosystem potential, reintroducing native trees and other perennials. This system of perennial 
land cover leads to increased resilience to cl imate change through an improved capture of 
precipitation (reduced storm run- off) and a more diverse production system that reduces the 
risk of catastrophic crop failure. 
18. The project will build institutional capacity to  support this model of so-called analogue 
forestry in government agencies at local and na tional levels. Given that the approach is based 
principally on local ecosystem properties and capa cities, upscaling to other areas in Sri Lanka, 
with the increased capacity in, for instance, exte nsion services, is relatively straightforward. 
19. The increased and improved streamflow – larg er volume of streamflow, over a longer 
period into the dry season, with a lower sediment load – benefits the downstream users in the 
Mahaweli irrigation system. A PES scheme will be established through which the reservoir 
operators transfer funds to the land users in the project area that are demonstrably managing 
their land in a way that benefits downstream water users. This would be the first PES scheme at 
scale undertaken in Sri Lanka. 
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                            Scale:	High  
20. The project will introduce analogue forestry as an integrated land use system revolving 
around indigenous tree species assemblages, reaching maximum ecosystem productivity 
yielding a variety of commercially interesting crops, such as vegetables, fruits, herbs, tubers, 
medicinal plants and mushrooms. These are im portant co-benefits for the participating 
smallholder farmers, as the economic return on  these crops is better than the current annual 
crops and the exposure to catastrophic crop loss  due to climate variabil ity or pests (which may 
themselves be driven by climate change) is much  reduced through the production of multiple 
crops.


---
*Page 137*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 137 
 
 
21. The project introduces a PES scheme that makes a sustainable flow of financial 
resources available to the project beneficiaries in the project area. 
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                             Scale:	Medium  
22. Sri Lanka has experienced a remarkable economic development since the end of its civil 
war. Gross domestic product has grown exponentia lly, and poverty levels have dropped in line 
with this. That notwithstanding, Sri Lanka has limited fiscal space to undertake projects of this 
kind due to a high level of indebtedness. 
23. Sri Lanka is experiencing significant impact s from climate change, specifically through 
changing precipitation patterns. The main agricu ltural production areas are highly dependent 
on a stable supply on irrigation water, wh ich this project is helping to supply. 
3.5 Country ownership                                                                  Scale:	High  
24. The funding proposal aligns with all the national policies on climate change and the 
nationally determined contribution submitted to  the United Nations Framework Convention on 
Climate Change secretariat. During a country visit of the GCF Secretariat to Sri Lanka, the delegation was received by the President of Sri Lanka, also acting as Minister of Environment; the President stressed the importance of the sustainable management of the central highlands 
as an ecological cornerstone for all other areas of the island. 
25. The funding proposal was developed largely with national counterparts – the main 
intervention of analogue forestry was develope d by an internationally recognized Sri Lankan 
academic. 
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                       Scale:	Medium‐High  
26. The project requires a substantial investment in the form of grants, but there are many 
beneficiaries, including in downstream project areas. The overall cost-effectiveness of the 
project is demonstrated by an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 21 per cent over 20 
years. The benefit streams result from the redu ced costs as a result of erosion and sediment; 
increased income from higher cr op intensity; increased household income from better quality 
and marketing of products, community forestry and tourism; and reduced compensation due to 
fewer disasters. However, these calculations sti ll need to be verified when the Secretariat 
receives the detailed economic model. The mitiga tion benefit calculated over the total project 
budget is USD 25.30/tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent. 
27. The level of concessionality can be partially justified by market failures and the 
provision of public goods. Outp ut 2.2, the establishment of a PES scheme, provides a continuous 
source of finance for the project area, which is  considered to be an important benefit to 
safeguard the investment. This output directly addresses a market failure, and outputs 1.1, 3.1 
and 3.2 have characteristics of public good. Outputs 1.2 and 2.1 concern private enterprise development, although the accredited entity (AE)  has argued that this development is essential 
to produce the public good of  preserving the upstream catchm ent. Further review of the 
economic model will be necessary to see if the value of these instances of public good justify the requested grant element for these activities.  
IV. Assessment 	of	consistency 	with	GCF	safeguards 	and	policies	
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards


---
*Page 138*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 138 
 
 
28. The AE has completed a screening procedure and classified the project as having 
moderate risk. The Secretariat confirms this is eq uivalent to GCF’s environmental and social risk 
category B. The project aims to generate resilien t livelihoods by increasing the adaptive capacity 
to climate induced changes in critical upstre am and downstream rural communities of the 
Knuckles mountain range areas in Sri Lanka.  The activities are focused towards land 
management for irrigation agriculture, plan tations and forest reserves; promotion of 
sustainable and green value chains; payment for ecosystem services; and strengthening institutional capacity for land management.  
29. The AE has developed an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) 
that provides a platform for th e development and operation stages of the project to effectively 
and efficiently manage environmental and social issues and concerns. An ESMF has been developed as the specific sites that have to be identified and developed in detail together with 
the relevant local-level stakeholders will only be decided during the project implementation 
stage. The ESMF has described the environmental and social context of the area, including water 
resources, soil and forest areas. It has d escribed the socio-economic context, including 
population, ethnic distribution, income and ed ucational demographics. The ESMF was disclosed 
in English and a Non-Technical Summary was disclosed in Sinhala and Tamil languages. 
30. The ESMF provided provided an analysis of the relevant national policy and regulatory 
framework in the country and identified measures to ensure project’s compliance with the AE’s 
and GCF environmental and social safeguards. It  also identified potential environmental and 
social risk and impacts based on the proposed activities. Among these include the potential for erosion and sediment movement during streamside  protection and drainage management along 
roads especially if done during the rainy season . The rehabilitation and establishment of village 
tanks, ponds and irrigation networks could also lead to loss of vegetation and top-soil causing erosion as well as expose the community member s to possible accidents and water-borne and 
water-related diseases. The increase in cropping intensity might also lead to decrease in surface 
water quality through increased use of fertiliz ers and pesticides. The possible environmental 
and social risks and impacts that are identified  have been provided with mitigation measures 
through a preliminary risk management plan. Wh ile landscape forest restoration interventions 
could lead to temporary restrictions to access an d use of forest land and resources, the ESMF 
provided for a mechanism whereby decision s are taken voluntarily by those who are 
collectively using the area and resources for thei r livelihood. An action plan to mitigate the 
impacts from access restrictions will be developed should access restrictions be considered 
necessary. 
31. The ESMF included procedures for identifyin g and managing environmental and social 
risks of sub-projects. This comprises having a criteria that will guide in the site selection, 
reference to an exclusion list that describes th e sub-projects that are considered high risk 
projects and would not be supported, a scr eening procedure, a due diligence procedure 
including impact assessment particularly for mo derate risk sub-projects and clearance for low 
risk sub-projects, and development and implementation of mitigation measures through an 
environmental and social management plan incl uding its monitoring and supervision. The AE 
undertook screening to determine the presence of indigenous peoples, particularly Vedda 
people, and found that there is no presence of Vedda people in the project's area of influence. Additional examination is planned during the social baseline study and associated community consultations to analyze the presence of any ethn ic groups present in the sites that meet both 
the AE and GCF's broader definition of indige nous peoples. The examination will take into 
account the impacts to communities of any restrictions planned as part of the reforestation 
process and ensure that alternatives are provided. 
32. The AE will delegate an ESMS officer who wi ll be responsible for the overall supervision 
of the ESMF implementation in accordance to the AE’s role as implementing and supervising 
agency. It will be complemented by the project team/Project Management Unit (PMU) hosted at


---
*Page 139*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 139 
 
 
the AE’s country office. The executing entities wi ll be responsible for the implementation of the 
respective sub-projects as well as in implementing  the mitigation measures at the site level. A 
safeguards training on the ESMF for all project staff and relevant project partners are planned to be conducted during the inception phase of the project. An indicative budget for implementing the ESMF is also provided. 
33. Stakeholder consultations with government agencies, civil society organizations and 
communities were undertaken throughout the proj ect design process. Provisions for continued 
stakeholder engagement and information disclosu re are described in the project’s stakeholder 
engagement plan. The ESMF also specifies requirements for the project-level grievance 
mechanisms to be set up at the start of the project and that will complement the project complaints management system of the AE. 
4.2 Gender policy  
34. The proposal contains a gender assessment and gender action plan; therefore, it 
complies with the GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan. The assessment provides information on the enabling envi ronment in Sri Lanka to promote gender equality and women’s 
empowerment; voting and education rights being well-established in the country. The national 
policy and legal framework for promoting gender equality in Sri Lanka includes a women’s rights bill and international instruments for assuring women's rights, which have been ratified though both need either approval or incorporatio n into national legislation. Sri Lanka has also 
established the Ministry of Women and Child Affa irs, and a policy on women has been drafted. 
The gender assessment describes the gender issues at a national level in terms of access to healthcare, education, participation in the labo ur market (which for women generally offers 
limited economic opportunities due to low paying jobs in laborious industries, agriculture and 
informal sectors), and low participation in poli tical decision-making for women. Discrimination 
against women is also prevalent in the areas of inheritance and property rights given the patriarchal nature of cultural norms and belief s, which in turn, holding women and girls back 
from getting equal opportunities and benefits from overall development. 
35. The gender assessment also provides an analysis of gender issues in agriculture, natural 
resource management, and small and medium enterprises (SMEs). The assessment indicates 
that there are designated roles and responsibilities for women and men, in irrigated rice, 
horticulture and home gardens, while it indicates concerns in the livestock, plantations and forest and natural resource management. In the later activities, it indicates that women have 
more influence over home gardens given that pr oduce is mainly used at the household level. 
However, women seem to have less access to capital, inputs, extension services and market 
information for agricultural purposes, while are paid lower wages when wo rking as laborers in 
plantations. Regarding activities related to for ests, men are involved in timber harvesting and 
collection of other high-value forest products while women collect non-timber forest products 
for household use. Though both women and men can be in forest management committees, 
women play a greater role in monitoring, and th ey also participate in forest rehabilitation 
works. However, the assessment indicates that women are unseen contributors to forest 
protection and therefore, in fact , have limited direct power in forest protection activities. In 
relations to SMEs, women face challenges such as lack of access to finance, financial literacy, negative norms and practices, limited mobility an d unequal share in household responsibilities. 
The assessment also articulates the vulnerabilities women face as a result of climate change, for 
example, diminished access to water in drie r conditions. The design of the project has 
mainstreamed gender issues across the various components. The mainstreamed activities have considered interventions which build on women’s  already existing capacities and knowledge as 
well as increasing opportunities for women to  engage in other important forestry and 
agricultural related activities that will have re turns to development of themselves, communities 
and families. An additional and important expected  benefit that the assessment points out is the


---
*Page 140*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 140 
 
 
positive nutritional returns to women’s enhanced engagement. The assessment clearly 
demonstrates these considerations.   
36. The proposal contains a Gender  Action Plan outlining activities and tentative indicators 
with sex-disaggregated targets that are aligned wi th the project components. The Gender Action 
Plan is aligned with the logic framework in setting disaggregated targets. The action plan 
includes many activities that are aimed at addressing various challenges that women are expected to face in effectively engaging in the project. These activities include, but are not limited to, the followings: opportunities for women to engage in paid-labours; active 
engagement of women in the design, developm ent and management of farmers’ woodlots; 
engagement in water quality measurement and monitoring of the irrigation water use 
efficiency; opportunities for women to increase income through commercialization of their 
production; allocation of underutilized land to women, including female-headed households; 
development of women’s entrepreneurial skills on identification of market opportunities 
product development and marketin g; skills and knowledge deve lopment for women in tourism 
sector; and enhanced opportunities through the Green Listing process. The action plan also 
includes capacity development initiatives targeted  at various groups on mainstreaming gender, 
developments of tools and collectio n of sex disaggregated data.   
37. The Gender Action Plan is however expected to be fine-tuned following identification of 
the sites for the intervention and completion of the rapid social analysis and in-depth 
consultations with stakeholders. The AE will ensure that the social baseline analysis and collection of data are conducted prior to commencing implementation of the project.  The baseline, targets, indicators and timelines are therefore also expected to be refined as needed 
for all the activities, and those that are already in cluded are to be updated based on the findings. 
The PMU and the gender specialist that will be hired will be responsible for the implementation of the Gender Action Plan. Budget has been allocated for the implementation of the Gender 
Action Plan.  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall	programme 	assessment 	(medium 	risk)		
38. The funding proposal requests a GCF grant of USD 39.7 million, accounting for 79 per 
cent of the total financing. The total project cost  is USD 48.9 million with co-financing by way of 
grants from the government entities of Sri Lanka. There is no co-financing from the AE.  
39. There are three executing entities (EEs) namely, the International Union for 
Conservation of Nature (IUCN) Sri Lanka Country Office, MMDE and the International Centre for 
Research in Agroforestry (ICRAF ). All three components involve two EEs and five out of six 
outputs will be implemented by two EEs. While the AE has described project activities, some 
specifics will be finalized during the implementa tion. This may impact the livelihood of the 
beneficiaries. For instance, the project may need potential short-term restrictions on the use of 
natural resources; and specific intervention lo cations for ground activities have yet to be 
finalized. These can only be determined during project implementation according to the AE. The 
success of the project implementation depends on  efficient coordination among the AE, EEs and 
other relevant stakeholders to ensure transparen cy and equity with respect to access to the 
project benefit. 
4.3.2.  Accredited 	entity/executing 	entity	capability 	to	execute	the	current	programme 	
(medium 	risk)		
40. IUCN has operated in Sri Lanka for over 20  years and has worked with the country’s 
government. The IUCN Asia Regional office and IUCN Headquarters will be responsible for


---
*Page 141*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 141 
 
 
oversight of the project and ensuring its progr ess. The IUCN Sri Lanka Country Office will play 
the role of EE.  
41. The three EEs have a track record in projec t implementation. The MMDE is a national 
designated authority and the project manage ment unit (PMU) will be established in the 
ministry. IUCN Sri Lanka will provide technical services and mana ge most of the activities based 
on its experience and expertise. ICRAF will prov ide technical advice for components 2 and 3. 
The AE carried out the assessment of the procurement and financial capacity of MMDE and ICRAF and confirmed that the EEs have the re quired capacities, experience and policies. 
4.3.3.  Programme ‐specific	execution 	risks	(medium 	risk)		
42. Finalization of specific locations for interventions: the funding proposal aims to support 
a rainwater harvesting infrastructure (activity 1.1.2) and irrigation system for agriculture 
(activity 1.2.1). It is estimated that 1,084 villag es will benefit from these activities. However, the 
specific locations for interventions at the ground level will be finalized during the 
implementation after consultation with communities.  
43. Conflicts among stakeholders and discrimination against certain groups: the funding 
proposal outlined as risks potential conflicts among stakeholders and discrimination against certain groups over project benefit sharing, acce ss to project resources and land use planning at 
community and government levels. Other risk s are related to corruption, collusion and 
nepotism, and endangering the good governance practices as identified by the AE. The AE suggested multiple mitigants, such as setting up clear eligibility criteria, creating a multi-
stakeholder platform, engaging in continuous co nsultation, and the promotion of transparent 
and participatory practices. It will also focu s on areas where communities have a history of 
collaboration and conflict resolution for its init ial project implementation. Given that specific 
locations for interventions still to be finalized as mentioned above, the AE and EEs are relied 
upon to execute mitigants and monitor such risks during the project implementation.  
44. Potential restriction on the use of natural r esources: the AE identified a potential need 
for short-term restrictions on the use of natural resources as a risk because it might affect the livelihood of the local communities. It can be only determined during the project implementation. The AE already identified mitigants through the Standard on Involuntary Resettlement and Access Restrictions as well as a shared management mechanism between communities and the respective government agency.  
45. Economic viability: section F.1 of the fundin g proposal includes the economic analysis 
with a number of assumptions and five independ ent benefit streams identified based on the 
results framework to compute the values. The anal ysis yielded an EIRR of 21 per cent based on 
benefits estimated by AE and lifespan of benefits over 20 years under the base case scenario. 
Sensitivity analysis was undertaken, and the worst-case scenario assumed a 10 per cent 
increase in cost and a 10 per cent reduction in benefit at the same time, which resulted in the EIRR of 15 per cent. 
4.3.4.  Compliance 	risk	(medium 	risk)		
46. This is a project addressing water needs in the subject territorial area. In response to 
inquiries from the compliance team regarding potential risks that would make the project 
vulnerable to money-laundering, terrorist financing or prohibited practices, the AE responded that the project will have several safeguards in place to counteract these potential risks and threats.  
47. The overall governance structure of the proj ect will be provided by the government’s 
MMDE through the Project Board with the Secretary of MMDE, who is also the Chief Accounting Officer chairing the Project Board (or the Nati onal Steering Committee (NSC)). The project will


---
*Page 142*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 142 
 
 
be subject to AE oversight, with audits being conducted for each EE. The AE has reviewed the 
potential for political influence as a potential thre at but has indicated that this risk is mitigated 
and managed as the project will be governed by  a mixture of representa tives of government 
agencies, non-governmental organizations and ot her organizations, thus providing a broad base 
for accountability. The annual work plans and the implementation will involve extensive 
progress tracking and reporting, which will high light any mismanagement or misuses. The AE 
has also acknowledged and evaluated the risk of potential disruptions from political or natural 
disaster domains.  
48. The project design is highly technical, and the involvement of handouts are not within 
the project. Therefore, the short-term driven political interest would be minimal in the assessment of the AE. Nevertheless, the educatio n and advocacy work within the project will 
attract political authorities who are interested in long-term development. They would add value 
in terms of mainstreaming the approaches and ensuring sustainability by taking the message to 
the central government (national budget). In terms of natural disasters as described in the 
feasibility report, the project area is prone to drought and landslides. Dr oughts, if persistent, 
will impact the water availability for land us e improvements, including new plantings. Sri 
Lanka’s disaster response capacity is significan t and, therefore, disasters will not disturb the 
project much. Having reviewed the project proposal and the AE assessment of risk and mitigation measures, Compliance assigns this project with a medium rating. 	
4.3.5.  GCF	portfolio 	concentration 	risk	(low	risk)		
49. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains 
immaterial and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single 
proposal.

4.4 Fiduciary 
51. The EEs for the project are MMDE; ICRAF, Na irobi, Kenya; and IU CN Sri Lanka. Each 
activity will have a responsible/accountable EE to ensure the timely delivery of the activity and 
coordinate with other relevant activities. When an EE does not have the technical and delivery 
capacity of an activity or a portion of an activity, the EEs may opt to engage/outsource/contract a Partner entity (Government, Non-Government or  Private), including Universities. For the sake 
of clarity, the EE responsible for a given activity  is accountable for the delivery of the activity 
even if another EE is contributing technically to the same activity.

52. IUCN will play the dual role of EE and AE in the project. As EE, IUCN Sri Lanka will 
function as an in-country entity based on its host country agreement, while as an AE the IUCN 
Headquarters and Asia Regional Office will provide oversight on the project and cover other AE functions, such as:  
(a) Entering into contractual agreements with EEs;  
(b) Managing and disbursing GCF funds to EEs after providing no objection to workplans 
and budgets;  
(c) Reviewing financial expenditures and progress reports;  
(d) Overseeing project implementation in accordance with the project document and annual 
work pans and budgets, agreements with co-f inanciers and each executing agency rules 
and procedures;  
(e) Providing technical guidance to ensu re quality in project activities;  
(f) Providing financial reports to GCF;  
(g) Ensuring that the project complies with the terms agreed in the project’s respective 
funded activity agreement (FAA) as well as  the accreditation master agreement (AMA) 
signed with GCF; and  
(h) Undertaking regular supervision missions in accordance with IUCN guidelines. 
53. An NSC, comprising key entities of which IUCN will also be a member, will provide 
strategic guidance and financial approvals for pr oject implementation. In addition, the NSC will 
be responsible for the governance of the project and oversight of the PMU to ensure delivery of 
expected results. The EE, MMDE, as secretary to  the NSC, will chair the Committee, which will 
provide implementation guidance and support as  well as financial and procedural oversight. 
Furthermore, IUCN will enact financial contracts and transfers with actors executing the project, 
with consolidated financial and technical reporting through the PMU that will be established 
under the MMDE. IUCN will carry out financial tr ansfers in accordance with requests from the 
PMU, governed by the GCF procedural requirem ents. Furthermore, IUCN will enact financial 
contracts and transfers with ac tors executing the project, with consolidated financial and 
technical reporting through the PMU that will be  established under the MMDE. IUCN will carry 
out financial transfers in accordance with requests from the PMU, governed by the GCF 
procedural requirements.  
54. The PMU will be headed by the Programme Director, who is responsible for the day-to-
day operations within the guidelines laid down by the NSC, including the tasks of managing and 
monitoring the project risks. The remaining PM U staff will be resourced with professional and 
technically qualified personnel selected by th e NSC-appointed panel for the MMDE to approve. 
Hiring of successful candidates will be carried out by IUCN and ICRAF based on the terms of 
reference of each position. The PMU will develop the contracts, guidelines and technical documentation to engage and support sub-basi n integrated delivery teams, implementing 
partners and community level innovation platforms. 
55. The financial management between the AE and EEs of the project will be governed by 
the IUCN finance manual and policy. Funds will be transferred to IUCN according to the AMA 
and the FAA for the project and will be kept in a bank account specifically for the project. The 
IUCN Headquarters’ Global Finance Unit will ma nage fund disbursements to the PMU based on 
semi-annual work plans agreed by the IUCN superv ision team at the regional office. The PMU is 
then responsible for transferring the funds to the EEs and the services providers or suppliers. 
The PMU is also responsible for the accounting and fiduciary management of all funds disbursed 
and will adopt IUCN accounting systems. Furthe rmore, the PMU will be audited independently 
on a yearly basis.

4.5 Results monitoring and reporting 
57. This is a project that seeks transformation at ambitious scales, building on approaches, 
tools, capacities and a political will that reduce risk of failure. The project aims to support the 
communities to improve the efficiency of water, land and energy uses, and engage in value-added products, minimizing pre-harvest waste, su ch as fertilizer and water and post-harvest 
losses. While doing so, the sustainability of  the project is ensured by applying the PES 
approaches to maintain infrastructure, such as st ream banks and roads, etc., and benefits to the 
community through value-added agriculture and to urism, allowing them to better understand 
the value of ecosystems for quality liveli hoods while meeting the climate challenges. 
58. It is an adaptation project that is meant to protect 411,564 ha of upland and lowland 
agro-systems. The project is also estimated to have the capacity to sequester 1.9 million tCO 2eq 
over a period of six years from improved fore st management and land use. The project is 
expected to impact an anticipated 1.074 million  (100 per cent) beneficiaries (51.3 per cent 
female and 48.7 per cent male), including 268,643 direct beneficiaries (138,003 female and 130,640 female)  located in the intervention ar ea. Indirect beneficiaries of the project are 
estimated at 522,561 males and 552,012 females.  
59. The project will contribute to achieving three fund-level impacts, namely:  
(a) Increased resilience and enhanced liveli hoods for the most vulnerable people, 
communities and regions;  
(b) Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security; and  
(c) improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services. 
60. Regarding the theory of change diagram, this shows a clear causal linkage between the 
barriers and proposed intervention with clear reference to assumptions and risks. 
61. Regarding the timetable of implementation, earlier comments have been addressed and 
the project now conforms with the standard GCF format.  
62. For the logical framework, the revised fund ing proposal incorporated most of the 
comments and suggestions provided by the Secretariat and already conforms with GCF 
performance measurement frameworks and risk management frameworks.  
63. The monitoring and evaluation section of the funding proposal contains the required 
information and is aligned with the relevant GCF policies and guidelines. 
4.6 Legal assessment 
64. The Accreditation Master Agreement was sign ed with the Accredited Entity on 11 
October 2016 and it became effective on 11 January 2017. 
65. The proposed project will be implemented in the Democratic Socialist Republic of Sri 
Lanka, a country in which GCF is  not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, 
amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriat ion in this country,


---
*Page 145*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 145 
 
 
which risks need to be further assessed. GCF provided the government of the Democratic 
Socialist Republic of Sri Lanka with a draft privileges and immunities agreement on 18 March 
2016. The draft agreement is currently under ne gotiation, with the latest comments from the 
Democratic Socialist Republic of Sri Lanka having been received by GCF on 7 June 2019. 
66. The Heads of the Independent Redress Me chanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the coun try, or has been provided with a ppropriate privileges and immunities.

Independent 	Technical 	Advisory 	Panel’s	assessment 	of	
FP124	
Proposal name: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Subsistence Farmers and 
Agricultural Plantation Communities residing in the vulnerable 
river basins, watershed areas and downstream of the Knuckles 
Mountain Range Catchment of Sri Lanka 
Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) 
Project/programme size: Small 
I. Assessment 	of	the	independent 	Technical 	Advisory 	Panel	
1.1 Impact potential       Scale:	Medium  
1. The funding proposal is a small-scale projec t with a lifespan of six years that was 
developed and submitted to GCF for financial support by the Government of Sri Lanka together with the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), the accredited entity (AE). A 
USD 39.78 million grant has been requested by  the country from GCF for climate change 
adaptation activities, and to leverage USD 9.2 milli on in co-financing (18. 8 per cent of the total 
project cost) from the Government of Sri Lanka. 
2. This is the second submission from the AE elaborated based on the findings identified 
by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) in a previous submission. The revised 
funding proposal and additional annexes (annexes:  16(a) – project site climate analysis focused 
on rainfall deficit; 16(b) – project site climate analysis focused on the trends of potential evapotranspiration and moisture index; and 17 – hydrologic and climate analysis) are provided 
in this submission. Improvements made are highlighted throughout the report. 
3. The proposal targets ecosystems (biodiversity and water resources) in the 
Knuckles/Amban Ganga highlands (upstream catchment) and lowlands (downstream catchment). According to the information provided  in the funding proposal, the Knuckles region 
features mountains, valleys and a variety of ecosys tems and species. Its importance is reflected 
in the level of protection and international recogn ition that has been accorded at various times. 
The Knuckles Conservation Forest,
1 together with the Peak Wilderness Protected Area and 
Horton Plains National Park (collectively referred to as the Central Highlands of Sri Lanka) was 
declared a United Nations Educatio nal, Scientific and Cultural Or ganization World Heritage Site 
in 2010. Aside from its scenic beauty, the Knuckl es region is a vital watershed in the Mahaweli2 
drainage system. It constitutes 30 per cent of th e watershed forest of the Mahaweli catchment, a 
major source of water for the country’s agricultural sector. The funding proposal considers that 
these features make the Knuckles Conservation Fore st an ideal site to introduce the IUCN Green 
Listing process.3 
 
1 In 2000 the Knuckles area was declared a Conservation Fo rest by the Forest Depart ment, and in 2007 its buffer 
zone was declared an Environmental Protection  Area under the National Environmental Act. 
2 Mahaweli is the largest river in Sri Lanka; the newly commissioned Moragahakanda and Kaluganga reservoirs also 
depend on the watershed of the Knuckles Conservation Forest. 
3 IUCN Green Listing is a green growth  programme/activity. It focuses on branding and promotion of the site’s 
commitment to certified conservation against standards and with this certification contributes to monitoring the 
conservation process.


---
*Page 147*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 147 
 
 
4. The feasibility study showed that two monsoon seasons determine the climate 
seasonality of Sri Lanka (the monsoon and inte r-monsoon seasons). Sri Lanka’s territory is 
characterized by three climatic zones: “wet zone”, “dry zone” and “intermediate zone”. 
According to the records of the Department of Me teorology, a shifting in the climatic regions has 
been observed in the period from 1960 to 2010, making  the downstream catchment of the 
project area drier due to the expanding dry zone, and the wet zone becoming the intermediate zone. This shifting of the climate zones will further aggravate the existing water deficit in the 
area.  
5. The feasibility study also showed that rain fall, temperature and wind are key climatic 
parameters impacting the territory of Sri Lanka, particularly the agriculture and water sectors. 
The people and economy of Sri Lanka are heavil y dependent on water for irrigation, domestic 
and industrial use, and electricity generation. Rainfall in the wet and intermediate climatic 
zones of the country is collected in an intricate series of reservoirs and delivered to the dry zone 
agricultural lands. The water colle ction and delivery system is threatened by reservoirs silting 
up at alarming rates. One of the reasons for increa sing siltation in water-collecting reservoirs is 
increased soil erosion in upstream catchments, pa rtly intensified by increased rainfall intensity 
during the monsoon seasons. 
6. As regards the causes of siltation, a concern was raised by the independent TAP during 
the first submission. In particular, information about the potential share of climate change in 
increasing the siltation of reservoirs versus in effective and unsustainable maintenance of the 
system was not provided in the funding proposal. This information is crucial in decision-making 
on the potential impact of planned measures (e.g. to direct more effort and resources to land 
management or recommend measures for effective management of the maintenance of irrigation systems). Such information would have an impact on proper pl anning of the project 
exit strategy and ensure the long-term sustainability of the results, relevantly increasing the 
impact of the funding proposal. 
7. In the new submission, the AE provided addi tional information on soil erosion causes 
and results of hydrological modelling of the Am ban Ganga basin (covering the project area) and 
experience from the adjacent Upper Mahaweli  area for which more data are available 
(hydrologic analysis – annex 17). In its response , the AE highlights the difficulty of reliably 
separating the share of sediment yield attributab le to more erosive rainfall (driven by climate 
change) as opposed to soil erodibility (influen ced by land cover and human activities). As a 
result of hydrological modelling the AE reported  that sediment yield is 2.2 times more sensitive 
to rainfall erosivity than erodibility, indicating  that between 56 and 67 per cent of increased 
sediment yield is attributable to climate change. The AE also concludes that because of the 
spatial and temporal scale of the modelling possible  with available data, it is not unreasonable 
to categorize sediment yield as only becoming an  actionable problem because of climate change.  
8. The target activities of the funding proposal are: (1) land and water; (2) land 
degradation (erosion, leading to sedimentation); and (3) lack of water resources (in particular 
in the dry downstream catchment area). The fund ing proposal concept and its potential impact 
are described in paragraph 73 of the funding pr oposal. In particular, the paragraph states:  
(a) The project makes interventions in the upstre am area to help people adapt to climate 
change by managing their land and water more sustainably and profitably. This improves livelihoods of people in the upstre am catchment (200,331 female and 183,759 
male) while also contributing through environmental protection of the catchment 
(166,254 ha) to maintaining the irrigati on water supply to the downstream area 
(506,264 ha, 122,150 of which are irrigated rice), where the project intervenes with 
advice on climate resilient agriculture to ensure that efficient use is made of the water in 
downstream agriculture thereby benefiting  the very climate vulnerable dry zone 
population (489,684 female and 469,442 ma le). The project therefore has an overall 
reach of 672,518 ha and 1,343,216 people.


---
*Page 148*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 148 
 
 
9. The independent TAP is of the opinion that concentrating most of the activities in an 
upstream area might not be the most effective approach, taking into consideration that the 
downstream part of the catchment is much drier and has higher exposure to climate change. In 
its response to the independent TAP the AE has clarified that focus on the upstream catchment 
and sedimentation problem is a priority area decided by the government because the country has two other complimentary projects (United Nations Development Programme/GCF project implemented through the Ministry of Mahaweli  Development and Environment, and the World 
Bank funded project of USD 125 million implemented through the Ministry of Agriculture, see 
para. 69 of the funding proposal). Both proj ects contribute mainly to the downstream 
catchment area, improving rice agronomy through mo re efficient use of irrigation water in rice 
cultivation, improving the irrigation system itself and developing rural advisory services 
capacity. 
10. The funding proposal has three components, and each has six subactivities. For 
increased transparency and clarity of the conc ept and intervention considered, the measures 
planned in the funding proposal at the subactivity level are provided below: 
(a) Component 1: climate-resilient sustainable ma nagement of land and water (44 per cent 
of GCF financing):  
(i) Subactivity 1.1.1: streamside protection  and drainage management along roads. 
There are 2,373 km of streams and canals and 1,766 km of all types of roads in the upper catchment from which the pr oject intervention segments will be 
selected;  
(ii) Subactivity 1.1.2: rehabilitation and establishment of village tanks, ponds and 
irrigation networks. The output of this subactivity includes 32 abandoned village 
tanks and 121 functional ponds and tanks within the upper catchment area 
rehabilitated (de-siltation) and new tank s established, increasing the irrigation 
water availability in both upstream and downstream catchments; 
(iii) Subactivity 1.1.3: restoratio n of forest mosaic landscap es. This activity focuses 
on the following: restoring degraded for ests within protected areas and forest 
fragments; regrowing forests in priori ty areas for the supply of ecosystem 
services, especially watershed protection ; and the planting of trees outside 
forests for improved sustainability and li velihoods. A dense forest of 41,911 ha, 
an open forest of 21,905 ha, and a scrubl and (degraded grassland) of 62,204 ha 
will be rehabilitated. The project interventions will also improve the canopy 
structure of the vegetation in the upland areas, thus enhancing cloud 
interception and increased rain capture; 
(iv) Subactivity 1.2.1: development of r esilient farming techniques to grow 
appropriate climate adapted varieties and make efficient use of irrigation water 
and fertilizer (maximizing use of orga nic inputs and recycling) as well as 
employing integrated pest control based on real-time weather and pest incidence data (connected to the inform ation system developed in subactivity 
3.1.2). The output of this subactivity is that the cropping index
4 will increase in 
both the upstream and downstream catchments from 0.8 to 1.7 (a paddy of 122,150 ha downstream and of 8,793 ha upstream);  
(v) Subactivity 1.2.2: intensification of su stainable smallholder production in the 
upstream catchment (home gardens, analog forests,5 spice gardens and annual

independent TAP is related to the immediate effe ct and long-term sustainability of the results 
achieved through component 1 and its subactivities 1.1.1, 1.1.2 and 1.1.3. Two key results are 
anticipated from these subactivities: an increase in the availability of irrigation water for 
irrigation rice through rehabilitation of irrigat ion systems (cleaning silted reservoirs, building

new reservoirs, improving management of roads and canals/streams), and an increase in the

12. In a new submission the updated climate analysis (annex 16(b)) was provided by the 
AE. This analysis includes assessment of moisture  index trend by 2050, which shows that, while 
the dry zone project area will get drier in the key crop growing months of January and May (creating a median requirement for 104.48 mi llion cubic meters of irrigation water), 
corresponding increases in the moisture index in  the upstream project area from August to 
October (median increase in surplus water of 237.61 million cubic meters) could potentially 
compensate an increase in irrigation requiremen ts if rainfall intensity on sedimentation is 
reduced and storage reservoirs and water tran sfer channels are more protected from the 
impact of sedimentation, which is one of the focus area of the funding proposal along with an 
increase in rainwater collection capacity of the ecosystem.  
13. The new submission provides a much detailed, area-specific feasibility study on changes 
in climatic parameters driving the targeted sectors, but the results for assessment of water 
supply and demand trends, taking climate chan ge into consideration (and including the 
approach for prioritization of consumers duri ng decision-making) is not still provided.  
14. It is the opinion of the in dependent TAP that the funding proposal could have high 
impact on increasing the irrigation water supply in the downstream catchment, and ensure 
sustainable land management in upstream catchment in the Knuckles region of Sri Lanka. However, taking into consideration the ambiti ous plans of the Government of Sri Lanka to 
expand the irrigated paddy area, sustainability of  the impact will be limited in future. At this 
stage of the assessment a demonstration of su stainable management of the water supply 
process is not provided, but it will be implemente d as a condition to approval of the funding 
proposal. This capacity gap in long-term su stainable management of the water supply and 
demand process makes the impact score “medium”. 
1.2 Paradigm shift potential         Scale:	High  
15. The funding proposal aims at implementation of several transformational elements: 
(a) Component 2 (subactivities 2.2.1, 2.2.2 and 2.2.3) of the funding proposal supports 
establishment of a PES mechanism . Three potential income streams have already been 
identified in the funding proposal: a levy on hydropower generation; a levy on export 
crops used to incentivize best practices in plantations; and the use of general taxation 
receipts to incentivize smallholder farmers to adopt sustainable land-use practices, 
building on the existing system of welfare payments. The Government of Sri Lanka has 
already committed in the project proposal to  implement the hydropower levy as a PES 
mechanism. There is already a levy on export crops that pays  for support services to the 
sector, and the project plans to explore possibi lities to extend this to support catchment 
management together with other options for channelling some of the increased income 
envisioned from value chain upgrading under output 2.1 in both the plantation sector 
and amongst smallholder farmers to develop novel sustainable PES modalities from increased revenue associated with sustainable production; 
(b) One of the subactivities (2.1.2 ) enables the policy environment for use of Green Listing 
(for nature, culture and heritage elements) to enhance community income and engage the climate-affected vulnerable poor. Currently the beneficiaries are not engaged in the 
development of various social  welfare schemes created to support the vulnerable. This 
subactivity aims to leverage government  investments in subsidies and make it


---
*Page 152*
---

 
GCF/B.25/02/Add.01 	
Page 152 
 
 
compulsory for beneficiaries and vulnerable grou ps to be part of the green activities that 
generate income for them while supporting conservation. Connecting these groups with 
a PES mechanism would be more meaningful and sustainable in order to minimize 
subsidies. In addition, the project will prov ide the Green List label to local tourism 
providers, local stakeholders such as non- governmental or civil society organizations 
directly supporting the protected area to achieve successful conservation outcomes. This process should contribute to equitably sharing collected tourism benefits more 
centrally;  
(c) One more transformational element cons idered in the funding proposal is 
implementation of sustainable land management practices, mainly in the upstream 
catchment area, which will improve the climate change adaptive capacity of biodiversity, 
agriculture and irrigation systems; and  
(d) For strengthening the national and regional ca pacities in climate resilient, sustainable 
management of the vulnerable river basins in watershed areas and downstream of the 
Knuckles Mountain Range catchment area of Sr i Lanka the funding proposal plans to put 
in operation decision-making supporting tools for data collection and analysis. Tools required for climate pressure and water deficit analysis should include, as minimum, tools for assessment of trends of potentia l evapotranspiration, crop-related water 
deficit, moisture index, soil-water hy drology, water supply-demand (e.g. WEAP
8 
software or any other similar tool available) and others.  
16. In conclusion, the paradigm shift is assessed as “high” subject to successful 
implementation of conditions provided in secti on II. Despite the local nature of adaptation 
measures, the knowledge system and capacity established by the funding proposal for sustainable planning of land use and water resources in the Knuckles region could be replicated 
in other regions of Sri Lanka as well as at national level.  
1.3 Sustainable development potential        Scale:	High  
17. Sri Lanka’s national adaptation plan id entifies various adaptation options and 
performance indicators for the nine identified most vulnerable sectors. This funding proposal 
directly addresses four of the nine vulnerab le sectors: food securit y, water resources, 
ecosystems and biodiversity, and export development all of which contribute to sustainable development processes.  
18. Component 2 of the funding proposal aims to establish a PES mechanism to transfer 
value realized from using water derived from  the upstream catchment to fund catchment 
protection. As mentioned in the “paradigm shift” investment criterion section, three potential 
income streams have already been identified in  the funding proposal, a levy on hydropower 
generation, a levy on export crops used to incent ivize best practices in plantations and the use 
of general taxation receipts to incentivize sm allholder farmers to adopt sustainable land-use 
practices, building on the existing system of welfare payments. In case of successful 
implementation of these mechanisms they could significantly contribute to the sustainable 
management of ecosystems and strengthen ecosystem services. 
19. The funding proposal will contribute to several SDGs and their target areas:  
(a) SDG 15, “life on land”. The highest contribution  would probably be to this SDG, which is 
focused on forests, desertification and biodiver sity. One of the target areas of this SDG is 
to ensure the conservation of mountain ec osystems, including their biodiversity, to

(b) SDG 1, “no poverty”. The funding proposal aims  to build the resilience of the poor and

1.4 Needs of the recipient           Scale:	High  
21. Sri Lanka is facing a significant strain on  its economy. Despite significant economic 
success, largely driven by reconstruction and in creased consumption, there are deep regional 
disparities in wealth and well-being. Loans and borrowing that financed the post-war 
infrastructure expansion (roads, railways, housing)  created a serious balance of payments crisis 
and an onerous debt burden that need to be addressed by new macroeconomic policies. The key 
development challenge is to maintain the momentum of rapid inclusive and sustainable growth. 
22. Both upstream and downstream populations fa ce problems of food insecurity, which has 
been a stubborn indicator to shift in Sri Lank a (the World Food Prog ramme estimates that 22 
per cent of people in the country are undernouris hed) despite the overall progress of economic 
development. 
23. In the short term, therefore, the Government of Sri Lanka is constrained to fund 
resilience-related investments through domestic financing, especially the incremental 
investment required to build the coping ca pacity of vulnerable, rural communities. 
24. According to the funding proposal, climate-re lated hazards pose a significant threat to 
economic and social development in Sri Lanka. By  2050, potential impacts due to climate change 
are predicted to lead to an approximately 1.2 pe r cent loss of annual gross domestic product.  
25. According to the country’s NDC, various so urces confirmed that climate change could 
alter natural systems connected to the water cycle, the ecosystems and the biodiversity of the 
country, diminishing the different ecosystem servi ces. Food security, water and irrigation, and 
biodiversity are identified in the funding prop osal as the country’s adaptation priorities. 
Agriculture, livestock and fisheries are prioriti zed to ensure food security. Improvement of 
water collection and irrigation systems by increasing the sustainable management of 
biodiversity and land is a focus of the funding proposal and is in compliance with national 
adaptation priorities. A food security-related indi cator (e.g. the crop index) is also part of the 
funding proposal monitoring plan, and subactivit y 1.2.1 commits to increa se this indicator from 
0.8 to 1.7.  
26. In addition, the country has prioritized adaptation initiatives that drive mitigation co-
benefits. Restoration of forest mosaic landscapes is one of the planned adaptation activities, 
which could potentially contribute to the increasi ng of carbon stock (a mitigation activity).  
27. One of the focus areas of the funding proposal is sustainable water management 
practices for the central highlands and other marginal areas to minimize land degradation and 
improve land and water productivity, which is al so a priority action in Sri Lanka’s NDC.

28. For the agriculture sector, the NDC plans to  re-demarcate the agro-ecological region

29. It is the opinion of the independent TAP that  to improve the effect iveness of the funding 
proposal and ensure a higher impact, some resources allocated for component 3 of the funding proposal should be re-directed to re-demarcati ng agro-ecological zones and, at a minimum, 
assess changes in the agro-ecological parameters of the targeted project area. Based on the 
results of this reassessment, the risks of the planned measures should be evaluated and recommendations on the relevancy of traditiona l crops and approaches, or their replacement 
with other crops, should be issued. 
30. The high need for projects that seek to increase the availability of water is demonstrated 
here by the Government of Sri Lanka, which is submitting a second funding proposal from this 
sector. The first funding proposal focused on rehabilitation of drinking and irrigation water 
systems in Sri Lanka and is being implemented by the United Nations Development Programme, 
namely “Strengthening the resilience of sma llholder farmers in the Dry Zone to climate 
variability and extreme events through an integrated approach to water management” (FP0016).  
1.5 Country ownership          Scale:	High  
31. This funding proposal was developed together  with the Government of Sri Lanka, and 
the Government’s financial contribution to the project is 18.8 per cent of the funding, which will mainly contribute to activities 1.1.1, 1.1.2 and 3.2.2 as well as cost sharing during programme

downstream areas. This activity directly contributes to the governmental programmes 
for increasing rice production (pp. 40–41 of  the funding proposal include information on 
irrigation); and 
(f) The complete picture of the Government of Sri Lanka’s participation and contribution to 
the implementation process is provided  in the funding proposal (p. 54).

33. The funding proposal will contribute to the National Agriculture Policy of Sri Lanka to
